- **(2.14) Evals:** `readiness.json` gets a fourth workshop example ("movie watchlist with posters from Wikipedia (example)", title "Film Posters Watchlist", screens Find a film, Watchlist, Watched), so the build suite builds four (`--example <1-4>`); a new category "free service" (Open-Meteo weather board, Open Library book club; ready); new not-ready outside-service examples: a sign-in to a music service, an AI chat service, phone notifications. The old "weather dashboard with a live forecast" example became "stock board with the company's API key" (a live weather forecast from a free service is now ready). `clarify.json`: the movie example's `mentions` are now Wikipedia / free / no key / needs no key / public, and a new optional `avoids` list (any check) fails a reply that says "cannot reach", "not possible" or a technical word (API key, endpoint, JSON, CORS, URL). `guardrails.json` is unchanged (its outside-service ideas were already on-path and the company-key one a flag).
- **(2.14) Build eval network:** only the app's own origin is routed; everything else goes to the real network. Playwright's Chromium ignores `HTTPS_PROXY`, so behind a session proxy the eval launches it with `proxy: { server }` and opens the context with `ignoreHTTPSErrors` (the proxy re-signs TLS); without a proxy neither is set. A passing build's line names the outside hosts the page asked for. "No console error" stays.
- **Outside-service ideas go on to the guide, not the facilitator (fix, 2026-11-25; changes decision "(1.7) Guardrail check"):** Franck's test idea (a movie list with posters "through a free API") was held for a facilitator every time: guardrail-v1 flagged "an app that calls an external paid API or needs someone's API key or account", and the model reads any movie API as needing a key. `guardrail-v2`: an otherwise harmless idea that wants information from an outside website or service (free or paid API, posters, covers, weather, rates, maps) is on-path; flag keeps "needs someone's own API key, password or account (theirs or the company's)". `clarify-v3` adds one rule: apps cannot reach other websites or services; when an idea needs one, say so kindly in one sentence after the restatement and keep going with the version where people type the information in themselves, never stop or send them away. Spec item 4.1 ("no outside APIs") and the deploy check are unchanged; this only changes who explains the limit (the guide instead of a facilitator). Evals: `guardrails.json` gets an "outside service" category (Franck's message word for word plus weather/rates and an ISBN lookup, all on-path; the company-key weather example stays a flag), `clarify.json` an example whose first reply must name the limit (new optional `mentions` field). Live: guardrails 23/23, clarify 6/6.
- **(2.13) Reset keeps app files (Franck, 2026-11-25):** a full participant reset leaves the app's files in the apps bucket until the next build's deploy removes stale files; only the deployer's role may change app files, and that rule stays.
- **(2.13) Full reset command:** `npm run participant -- reset <nickname> [--yes]` (root `npm run -s participant -w platform --`, `platform/src/participant/`), run with `AWS_PROFILE=franck`. It composes the conversation reset: same argument parser, same "AI is answering" check (`aiAnswering`, now exported), same conditional restart transaction and batch deletes; `DynamoParticipantResetStore` extends `DynamoResetStore` (whose batch delete became `deleteKeys(pk, ...)`). `npm run conversation -- reset` keeps what it deletes, now also refuses during a running build (see "(2.13) Conversation reset refuses during a running build"), and its usage text points to the full reset.
- **(2.13) Reset order:** read and refuse first (unknown nickname, AI answering, build `queued`/`building`/`deploying`), then open GitHub (read `ssd-workshop-github`, mint the token, ask whether the repository exists) before any write, so a missing secret or an unreachable GitHub changes nothing. Then: restart (conversation item deleted and step `clarify`, one transaction conditional on the conversation being unchanged), messages and spec versions, live marker, app data (listed again after the marker is gone, deleted, then after a 2 s pause, `SETTLE_MS` with an injected `sleep`, listed and deleted once more), repository last. The step change goes first because without an approved spec no new build can start and recreate the repository or the live marker while they are deleted; the marker goes before the data because without it the data API answers 404 to every new request. The data API (ticket 2.4, unchanged) reads the marker apart from its write transaction, so a write that read the marker just before it went can still commit after the first listing: the second pass deletes what such a write left, and only a write held up longer than the pause (a stalled Lambda) could still land afterwards, a value with its totals in an app that is no longer live (accepted for test participants; a rerun deletes it). Every later step accepts "already gone", so a rerun repeats them all and finishes; a rerun after a stopped run also deletes messages the participant wrote in between (the conversation reset finds them as at any run). A participant who raced through approve and launch in the minutes between a stopped run and its rerun's repository delete, or between the restart and the repository delete of one run, would see that build fail when its repository goes (accepted for test participants).
- **(2.13) Running build found through the build link:** the refusal reads the conversation item's `buildId` and that build's `status`; a build record no conversation links to is not checked (only the readiness check starts builds, and it writes the link in the same transaction). A link to a missing build record does not block. Both resets refuse through the one check (`runningBuild` in `platform/src/conversation/command.ts`, `getBuildStatus` on `ResetStore`): if the conversation reset could delete the conversation item during a running build, it would drop the only link, and a later full reset would go ahead while that build recreates the repository and the live marker.
- **(2.13) Conversation reset refuses during a running build (operator-only behaviour change):** `npm run conversation -- reset` (dry run and `--yes`) now refuses, with the full reset's message, while the linked build is `queued`, `building` or `deploying`; before, it deleted the conversation item and its build link. Operator command only, nothing participant-facing changes. Its usage text says so and points to `npm run participant -- reset <nickname>`.
- **(2.13) Live marker line:** the reset's output says "deleted the live marker" or "the live marker was already gone" from what its own delete found (`DeleteItem` with `ReturnValues: ALL_OLD`), not from the read before, so a rerun after a stopped run reports what that run did.
- **(2.13) Participant before step 1:** a nickname whose profile has no step 1 yet (no step, or `nickname`) has no conversation to reset; its profile is left alone and its app data, live marker and repository (keyed by the claimed nickname) are still deleted. A participant with nothing left gets a run that says so and finishes (exit 0).
- **(2.13) GitHub token for the reset:** the organization-wide token with `administration: write` only (checked like the launcher's, `adminToken`), held inside `openRepositoryAdmin` and never returned or printed. Not a token limited to the one repository: GitHub refuses to mint one for a repository that does not exist, which would turn "already gone" into an error. `GET` 404 / `DELETE` 404 mean already gone; `DELETE` 204 deleted; anything else fails with a `GitHubProblem` naming the step and status.
- **(2.13) Dry run reads the secret too:** without `--yes` the command also opens GitHub (secret, JWT, installation, token) to say whether the repository exists; it changes nothing but needs the same credentials as the real run.
- **(2.13) Names printed:** the command prints the nickname and the repository's name (`ssd-workshop-apps/ssd-<nickname>`) to Franck, who typed the nickname; the rule "never log a repository name" is about GitHub worker code whose logs go to CloudWatch (`.claude/rules/platform.md` now says so). The GitHub call log entries are not printed. Never printed: email addresses, participant ids, tokens, the JWT, message or spec text, data values (tested).
- **(2.13) Error output:** only the error's name (`errorName`), except a `GitHubProblem`, whose message is written to be shown (step and status, or "the GitHub secret is not loaded"), printed after its name.
- **(2.13) Fake GitHub's refused delete:** the in-memory GitHub answers 404 to a `DELETE` without administration write (`statuses.test.ts` forbids the literal 403 in API sources); real GitHub's 403 fails the reset as a `GitHubProblem`, never as "already gone" in production.
- **(2.13) Kept items:** allow-list entry, nickname claim and profile nickname, sessions, `USAGE`, `BUILD#` records and `LAUNCHES` entries are never read or written by the reset. No new dependency (`@aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager` was already a platform dependency) and no infra change (Franck's own credentials).
- **(2.13) Proxy:** the AWS clients take the region and the session proxy as the other commands do; GitHub calls use Node's `fetch`, which ignores `HTTPS_PROXY`, so the command runs from Franck's machine (as `npm run smoke -- github`).
- **Paid services (Franck, 2026-11-23):** ECS Fargate, ECR, Secrets Manager and a public IPv4 address per running build task are approved for phase 2 (under $1 a month at rest; Bedrock about $0.50 to $2 a build, $20 to $50 for phase 2's tests). No NAT gateway.
- **Spec delta, CHANGED (Franck, 2026-11-23, PR #24):** section 5 "Code" and item 8: participants' repositories live in a free GitHub organization Franck owns for the apps (not a company organization, not his personal account), written through a GitHub App installed only there; each build gets a one-hour token for its one repository.
- **Access stack for build tasks (Franck, 2026-11-23):** `ecs:DescribeTaskDefinition` on `Resource *` is accepted as a new risk (the workshop's principals can read other projects' task definitions, environment included; check: CloudTrail); in return ticket 2.2 pins `RunTask`/`StartTask` to the workshop subnets (`ecs:subnet`), closing accepted risk 1.
- **Spec delta, CHANGED (Franck, 2026-11-23, PR #24):** section 5 "Builder ... has no AWS rights" becomes "The coding agent holds no AWS rights; the builder task may only call Claude, report progress and hand the result to the deployer." The coding agent is the Claude Agent SDK with file tools limited to its workspace (no shell, no network); the runner around it holds the task role (one Bedrock profile, its build's records, the deploy queue) and the one-repository GitHub token.
- **Apps sandboxed (Franck, 2026-11-23):** `/apps/*` responses carry `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox allow-scripts allow-forms allow-modals allow-popups` (no `allow-same-origin`): apps never see the platform's cookies and cannot call its API as their viewer; they have no `localStorage` and store data only through the data API, readable and writable by anyone with the app's address. Since 3.24 the sandbox also has `allow-popups-to-escape-sandbox` (decision "(3.24) Popups escape the sandbox"), and writes need an app write token.
- **GitHub organization (Franck, 2026-11-23):** `ssd-workshop-apps`, with the GitHub App `ssd-workshop-gh-app` installed on all its repositories (Administration and Contents read and write). Franck keeps the private key on his machine until ticket 2.6 creates the secret `ssd-workshop-github`.
- **Model quotas (Franck, 2026-11-23):** Franck requested Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 tokens-per-minute quotas; the platform uses the EU geographic profiles (`eu.anthropic.*`, spec: EU routing), whose quotas are separate from the global ones. Until granted, every role stays on Sonnet 4.6.
- **Models per role (2026-11-23, PR #24):** each AI role (guardrail, clarify, spec, readiness, build) has its own model and price setting, and every eval takes `--model <id>`; a role moves to a cheaper or newer Claude model only after its eval passes on it.
- **Other providers' models for benchmarks (Franck, 2026-11-25):** "if the access rules only allow Anthropic models, adapt them to allow others". The `Bedrock` statement of `ssd-workshop-deploy-edge` (Claude sessions and the deploy role) now allows any provider's foundation model and the `eu.*` and `global.*` inference profiles, so sessions can benchmark them for the builder role. The boundary is unchanged: the platform's roles still reach only Claude. Global profiles may process a request outside the EU (the spec asks EU routing for the platform; a benchmark sends only the example specs). Moving the builder to a non-Claude model would replace the Claude Agent SDK (spec delta, needs Franck's yes).
- **Builder without extended thinking (2026-11-25, build time and cost benchmark):** the coding agent runs with `thinking: { type: "disabled" }`. Benchmark on Sonnet 4.6 through the real runner, deploy check and browser check: the live gym tracker spec (four screens and a chart) took 237, 402 and 492 s and $0.80, $1.22 and $1.76 with thinking (the first file only after 2 to 5 minutes), against 102 to 127 s and $0.40 to $0.54 without; the three eval specs went from $0.30 to $0.47 to $0.17 to $0.19. An AI review of each app against its spec scored both the same (completeness 9, correctness 7 to 9). The live build (2026-11-25, 8 min, $1.64) matches the thinking runs.
- **Bedrock proxy accepts a model id with a colon (2026-11-25):** Claude Code sends the model id unencoded in the path (`/model/eu.anthropic.claude-haiku-4-5-20251001-v1:0/invoke-with-response-stream`), but the proxy accepted only the URL-encoded form, so a build model with a `:` (Haiku 4.5, Sonnet 4.5) was refused (403). The proxy now accepts both forms of the build model's paths and forwards the encoded one.
- **(2.1) Builds stack:** `ssd-workshop-builds` (eu-central-1, `infra/lib/builds-stack.ts`), inline template synthesizer (no file assets), no dependency on the other stacks. Names in `infra/lib/config.ts`.
- **(2.1) Network:** VPC `10.42.0.0/16` (an uncommon range, so a later peering or VPN is less likely to clash), two public `/24` subnets in `eu-central-1a` and `eu-central-1b`, one internet gateway, no NAT gateway, no VPC endpoint, no flow logs (a paid log for traffic that is HTTPS only). The subnets are named `ssd-workshop-builds-public-1` and `-2`.
- **(2.1) Fixed zones:** the zones are constants and the stack overrides `availabilityZones`; otherwise CDK asks AWS for the account's zones at synth time, which CI cannot do. A test checks the assembly needs no context lookup.
- **(2.1) No public IP by default:** `MapPublicIpOnLaunch` is off on both subnets; build tasks ask for one in `RunTask` (`assignPublicIp: ENABLED`), so nothing else started there gets a public address by accident.
- **(2.1) Default security group left alone:** `restrictDefaultSecurityGroup: false`. The VPC's default group is created untagged, so the deploy rights (`Ec2ChangeTaggedOnly`) cannot revoke its rules, and CDK's restriction needs a custom-resource Lambda; build tasks always name `ssd-workshop-builds`.
- **(2.1) Security group:** `ssd-workshop-builds`, one egress rule TCP 443 to `0.0.0.0/0` (GitHub, Bedrock, ECR, DynamoDB, SQS all speak HTTPS), no ingress, IPv4 only (the VPC has no IPv6).
- **(2.1) Cluster:** `ssd-workshop-builds`, Fargate only (no EC2 capacity), Container Insights off (paid metrics; the tasks' own logs suffice).
- **(2.1) Image repository:** `ssd-workshop-builder`, scan on push (the free basic scan), one lifecycle rule expiring all but the newest 5 images (tagged or not), mutable tags, retained on stack delete, no `emptyOnDelete` (a custom-resource Lambda) and no repository policy (denied by the access stack).
- **(2.2) The brief's RunTask subnet pin is not possible in IAM; a narrowed version was built; Franck chose it on 2026-11-24 ("Narrow it, keep risk 1 open"), with the new wording of criteria 1 and 3 in the 2.2 brief:** the AWS Service Authorization Reference (`servicereference.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/v1/ecs/ecs.json`, read 2026-11-24) says `ecs:subnet` exists on CreateService/UpdateService and CreateExpressGatewayService/UpdateExpressGatewayService; never on RunTask/StartTask (a negated condition on the missing key would deny every `RunTask`, a `ForAnyValue` one none). So accepted risk 1 is narrowed, not closed: services are pinned to the two subnets, `StartTask` is denied, and under the boundary only the launcher role starts tasks; the launcher role and the deploy principals can still `RunTask` into any subnet (CloudTrail check in `infra/access/README.md`). The `DescribeTaskDefinition` grant is built, but Franck accepted it in return for closing risk 1, and risk 1 is now only narrowed: the grant awaits his confirmation.
- **(2.2) Subnet ids:** `BUILDS_SUBNET_IDS` in `infra/lib/config.ts` (`subnet-05229d71b1e0b41ff` in `eu-central-1a`, `subnet-0965bcee41d455dba` in `eu-central-1b`, live since 2.1's deploy on 2026-11-24), written as literals in the access stack (it is applied by hand, without parameters for them); a test checks that the template and its README name exactly these.
- **(2.2) Services pinned with `ForAnyValue:StringNotEquals` on `ecs:subnet`** (deny `CreateService`/`UpdateService` and `CreateExpressGatewayService`/`UpdateExpressGatewayService`, every action carrying the key, when any subnet is not a workshop one; try 2 added the Express Gateway pair, which try 1 missed; the test checks the deny lists exactly the actions of its `ECS_SUBNET_ACTIONS` constant), in the deploy policies and the boundary: an update that does not change the network carries no subnet and must not be refused. A `CreateService` without subnets (no `awsvpc`) is not denied; Fargate services always carry subnets. The boundary copy is redundant today (it grants no service rights) and guards a future grant.
- **(2.2) `StartTask` denied outright** (deploy policies and boundary, `NoStartTask`): it places tasks on EC2 container instances, which the workshop never uses. The cluster deny now names only `RunTask`.
- **(2.2) Launcher role name:** `ssd-workshop-builds-launcher` on the path `/ssd-workshop/` (`BUILD_LAUNCHER_ROLE_NAME`/`_ARN` in `config.ts`); ticket 2.9 must create the launcher's role with exactly this name.
- **(2.2) Boundary `RunBuilds` pinned to the launcher** (`ArnEquals` `aws:PrincipalArn`, the role ARN with its path): no task role (participant code) or other workload role can start tasks, whatever its own policy grants. Unverified until 2.9's first build: that `aws:PrincipalArn` holds the path (AWS's SSO pattern `role/aws-reserved/sso.amazonaws.com/...` says it does). For 2.9 (access review, info only): this rule does not stop a confused deputy, so task roles get no write access to the launcher's trigger, the launcher validates what it runs, and the launcher role trusts only `lambda.amazonaws.com`.
- **(2.2) `ecs:TagResource` for the launcher** (`TagBuildsOnStart`, boundary): on `task/ssd-workshop-*/*`, only with `ecs:CreateAction` `RunTask` and only for the launcher role, because ECS checks it when `RunTask` passes `tags` or `propagateTags`.
- **(2.2) `ecs:DescribeTaskDefinition`** in the deploy policy's `ActionsWithoutResourceScope` (Resource `*` only), not in the boundary; the secret-reads test accepts it only for that statement and that exact action (`ACCEPTED_SECRET_READS`). Accepted risk 15 with its CloudTrail check; Franck accepted it in return for closing risk 1, which is now only narrowed, so the grant awaits his confirmation (see the first 2.2 line).
- **(2.2) `secretsmanager:GetRandomPassword`** (no resource scope, returns only a random string) added to `ReadsWithoutSecrets`: CloudFormation calls it with the caller's rights when CDK creates a secret without a value, as 2.6 does for `ssd-workshop-github`. No other right was found missing for 2.3 to 2.9: task definitions, `/ecs/ssd-workshop-*` logs, `ssd-workshop-builder` pulls, the `ssd-workshop-deploys` queue, the apps bucket, invalidations of tagged distributions, the secret and `PassRole` to ECS tasks are already covered. Not added: a deny of `RunTask` for the deploy principals (it would leave only the launcher able to start tasks, but Claude sessions could then not start a test build by hand); Franck can ask for it.
- **(2.3) Allowed file types:** `.html .css .js .json .md .txt .svg .png .jpg .jpeg .gif .webp .ico .woff .woff2` (`ALLOWED_EXTENSIONS` in `platform/api/build/check.ts`), matched in any letter case. Not allowed: files without a type (`LICENSE`, `Makefile` included), symlinks, anything that is not a plain file or folder, and hidden files and folders (any name part starting with a dot, `.gitignore` and `.git` included). `checkApp` checks every path it is given, `.git/...` included; only `readAppFolder` (2.8's working copy) leaves out the top-level `.git` folder, so it is neither checked nor counted.
- **(2.3) Safe names:** each part of a path holds only letters, digits, `-`, `_` and `.`, parts separated by `/`; absolute paths, `..`, empty parts, backslashes and spaces fail.
- **(2.3) `index.html` required** at the top of the app: an app without it fails the check (it would have no page to open). Not in the brief's criteria; added because the deployer would otherwise publish an app that shows nothing.
- **(2.3) Size:** at most 5 MB counted as 5 x 1024 x 1024 bytes over every file `checkApp` is given (a working copy's `.git` is left out by `readAppFolder`); the reason gives the size with one decimal ("The app is 5.2 MB; the limit is 5 MB."), never shown as 5.0 when it is over.
- **(2.3) Allowed addresses:** only `https://workshop.rankly.games` and any path under it (not `http://`, another port, a longer host name or a user part), plus the XML namespace names `http://www.w3.org/2000/svg`, `/1999/xlink`, `/1999/xhtml`, `/XML/1998/namespace`, `/2000/xmlns/` and `/1998/Math/MathML`, matched exactly (any letter case), which SVG, MathML and `createElementNS` need and are never fetched. No SVG DTD address: AGENTS.md says no DOCTYPE in SVG files. No GitHub "see the code" link: step 4 already links the repository, and one rule is simpler.
- **(2.3) Address scan:** every text file (`.html .css .js .json .md .txt .svg`), Markdown and comments included, line by line (lines split at `\r\n`, `\r` or `\n`), reporting file and 1-based line. Refused: any `name://` (or `name:\\`) address whatever the scheme; `http`, `https`, `ws`, `wss` or `ftp` followed by `:` and more text even without slashes (`http:host`; `{ http: 1 }` and `"https:"` pass); two or more slashes or backslashes in any mix followed by a host character, after a quote or backtick, `url(`, `=` (any attribute, a meta refresh's `url=`), a comma (later `srcset` entries) or a meta refresh's delay without `url=` (`content="0; //host"`, `"0 //host"`, `"0,//host"`), with spaces or control characters allowed between. Each line is read three ways and the first reading with a finding is reported: as written; with HTML entities (numeric, leading zeros included as in `&#0000047;` and `&#x00002f;`, and named `&colon; &sol; &bsol; &Tab; ...`) and JavaScript/JSON escapes (`\/`, `\u002f`, `\u{2f}` with any leading zeros, `\x2f`, legacy octal `\57`, and the control escapes `\t \n \r \v \f \b` as the real characters) decoded; with entities and CSS escapes (`\2f `, `\/`) decoded; tabs and line breaks dropped in all three (browsers drop them in addresses). Every reading applies to every text file, so a style block or script inside HTML is covered. `//` followed by a space or `*` (a comment) or in plain code (`a // b`) is not an address. Images and fonts are not scanned. The template's own Markdown therefore names no such address (the allowed namespace names excepted).
- **(2.3) Limits of the scan:** an address built from parts at run time (`location.protocol + "//host"`, `"/" + "/host"`, `atob(...)`) or split over lines cannot be found by a scan; the apps' CSP (below) blocks loading from it. Links (`<a href>` navigation) are not covered by the CSP, so only the scan guards those. A JavaScript comment right after a closing quote with no space (`'x'//note`) fails: telling it from an opening quote needs a tokenizer, and a quote count would open holes (template literals, escaped quotes); AGENTS.md says to start comments with `// `.
- **(2.3) Text must be UTF-8:** a text file that is not valid UTF-8 (`TextDecoder("utf-8", { fatal: true })`), starts with a UTF-16 byte order mark or holds a NUL byte fails with "It is not plain UTF-8 text, so it cannot be checked." (another encoding would hide addresses from the scan). A UTF-8 byte order mark is fine.
- **(2.3) Linear scan:** every pattern in the check starts only at the beginning of a run it would otherwise rescan (lookbehinds) and never puts two variable runs of the same characters side by side; a test scans 256 KB lines of letters, slashes, backslashes, digits, spaces, entities, escapes and meta refresh starts within a second each (the slowest, octal escapes, takes about 50-65 ms locally, so CI keeps a margin over 10x; time grows linearly with the length; try 1's pattern took 72 s on 400 KB).
- **(2.3) Data API contract for 2.4:** `workshop-data.js` sends `GET`/`PUT`/`DELETE /api/apps/<nickname>/data/<key>` to the same host (nickname from `location.pathname`), `credentials: "omit"`, the request and answer body being the JSON value itself (no wrapper), `content-type: application/json` on `PUT` (so browsers send a CORS preflight that 2.4 must answer), and `Origin: null` (the sandbox). A key with no value answers `GET` with `200` and the body `null` (not 404: Chromium logs every 404 as a console error, which the template's test refuses); `404` means the app (nickname) is unknown, and `workshop-data.js` then shows "This app is not live on the workshop yet, so it cannot load or save data."; any other error answer's `message` field is shown to the visitor. `DELETE` of a missing key answers `204`.
- **(2.3) Template app:** the starter shows a shared counter stored with `workshop-data.js`, so a fresh app proves the data API end to end; the coding agent replaces it. Classic `<script defer>` tags, not modules: under the sandbox a module script is refused by CORS (tested). `SPEC.md` in the template is a two-line placeholder that 2.6 overwrites whole with the approved spec.
- **(2.3) CSP constant:** `APPS_CONTENT_SECURITY_POLICY` in `infra/lib/config.ts`, used by the template's Playwright test now and by 2.5's headers policy.
- **(2.3) Spec delta, CHANGED (Franck, 2026-11-24, PR #28): apps CSP gets loading limits.** Old (decision "Apps sandboxed"): `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox allow-scripts allow-forms allow-modals allow-popups`. New: `sandbox allow-scripts allow-forms allow-modals allow-popups; default-src 'self'; connect-src 'self'; img-src 'self' data:; form-action 'self'`. Why: the deploy check cannot see addresses built at run time, and the sandbox alone lets an app load scripts, images and data from anywhere and send its data or visitors' input out. With the limits an app loads only its own files (images also from `data:`), sends requests and forms only to its own server, and runs no inline scripts or styles (AGENTS.md rule 11 says so; the template has none). Proved in Playwright under the sandbox (`template.test.ts`): `'self'` still matches the served address although the page's origin is opaque, so the template's files and its data round trip load; a fetch and an `<img>` to another origin are blocked and never reach it; an inline script does not run. Limit: proved only in Chromium; Safari's handling of `'self'` under `sandbox` is checked live in 2.5 (Safari on an iPhone, see "For 2.5"), not in CI.
- **(2.3) Tests and tooling:** the check's tests and the template's (Playwright under the sandbox CSP, the email and secret scan) live in `platform/api/build/` (`check.test.ts`, `template.test.ts`), so the root `tsconfig.json` typechecks them; `templates/` stays plain files outside the typecheck. ESLint lints `templates/**/*.js` as browser scripts with five globals declared inline in `eslint.config.mjs` (no `globals` package).
- **(2.3) No email rule in the check:** the check enforces the brief's three rules only; AGENTS.md tells the agent to put no personal data in the app, and the template is tested for email addresses and secrets. An email scan of every build is possible later if wanted.
- **(2.3) Problem list:** at most 50 problems (`MAX_PROBLEMS`) with files, in the order found, then the app's own problems (no `index.html`, the size), then one line "…and N more problems." (singular "problem" for one). Default, so a build with thousands of addresses gives a readable answer.
- **(2.3) Oversized apps:** the total size is counted first; over 5 MB the address scan is skipped (the app fails anyway), while names, types and `index.html` are still checked, so the participant sees every reason the scan does not need.
- **(2.3) For 2.5:** map file extensions to content types ignoring letter case (the check allows `PHOTO.JPG`); map tarball hard links and symlinks to `symlink` and every other non-file, non-folder entry to `other`; `\r`-only line endings are already split as lines by the check. `checkApp` lists at most 50 problems plus one "…and N more problems." line and skips the address scan when the app is over 5 MB, but it still gets the whole tarball in memory: the deployer records reasons on one DynamoDB item (at most 400 KB), so it caps the tarball's size (and the reasons' length) before calling `checkApp`. The live check in `infra/README.md` includes opening a live app in Safari on an iPhone: its styles apply and its data saves (the sandbox with `'self'` is proved only in Chromium).
- **(2.4) Live app marker:** an app is live when the item `APP#<lower-cased nickname>` / `LIVE` exists with a `liveAt` (ISO time; also `type: "app-live"` and `nickname` as typed, `AppLiveItem` in `platform/api/types.ts`, `appLiveKey` in `keys.ts`). Ticket 2.5's deployer writes it when an app first goes live; until then every app answers 404. The data API's role can only read it. For 2.5: keep the `APP#` partition holding only the `LIVE` marker (the data API's role may read any item under `APP#*`); other app state goes elsewhere, or that read gets narrowed first.
- **(2.4) Data in its own partition `APPDATA#<lower-cased nickname>`** (not `APP#<nickname>` as first suggested): values at `DATA#<key>` (`type`, `value` = canonical JSON text, `bytes`, `updatedAt`), totals at `DATA_USAGE` (`type`, `bytes`, `keys`, `updatedAt`, the daily write count `writeDay`, `writtenBytes`, and since try 3 each key's size in the map `sizes`). The role writes (Put, Update, Delete) only under `dynamodb:LeadingKeys` `APPDATA#*` and only the attribute names `pk sk type value bytes keys updatedAt writeDay writtenBytes sizes` (`APP_DATA_WRITE_ATTRIBUTES` in `infra/lib/config.ts`), and reads `APP#*` and `APPDATA#*`. Why: IAM cannot condition on the sort key, and a role with any write in the live marker's partition could replace or delete the marker (taking an app off, a denial of service) even without `liveAt`; IAM's `APP#*` never matches an `APPDATA#` key (tested).
- **(2.4) Nickname in the address:** matched lower-cased; one outside the 1.5 rules (3 to 20 letters, digits or dashes) or reserved answers 404 without reading the table, the same answer as a valid nickname with no live app.
- **(2.4) Keys:** `^[A-Za-z0-9_-]{1,64}$` (64 characters at most); any other key answers 400 "A key has 1 to 64 letters, digits, dashes or underscores." (after the live check, so an unknown app is always 404).
- **(2.4) Sizes:** 100 KB = 102,400 bytes and 1 MB = 1,048,576 bytes, counted as the UTF-8 bytes of each value's canonical JSON (`JSON.stringify` of the parsed body; keys and item overhead do not count). The body is refused with 413 before parsing when it is over 100 KB (over 136,536 characters when base64), and again when its canonical form is (e.g. `1e20` written out).
- **(2.4) Nesting:** at most 100 levels of lists and objects (counted on the text, without recursion), 400 "This value is nested too deeply: lists and objects can go at most 100 levels deep."; deeper values overflow the stack when written out, a failure that depends on the runtime.
- **(2.4) `null` can be stored:** `PUT null` stores the value `null` and takes a key; `GET` answers `null` for it and for a missing key alike. Invalid or missing JSON answers 400 "Send the value as JSON."; a `PUT` without `content-type: application/json` answers 415 (the shared message).
- **(2.4) Answers:** `PUT` and `DELETE` answer 204 without a body; `GET` 200 with the stored JSON text; the preflight (`OPTIONS`, a route of its own, the HTTP API keeps no CORS configuration) 204 with `Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, PUT, DELETE`, `-Headers: content-type, accept`, `-Max-Age: 600`, without reading the table; every answer, errors and 404 included, carries `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` and `cache-control: no-store`, never `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials` and never a cookie. CloudFront's `/api/*` behaviour needed no change (all methods, managed `SecurityHeadersPolicy` without CORS).
- **(2.4) Limit reasons (413):** "This value is too big: a value can hold at most 100 KB.", "This app has no room left: an app can store at most 1 MB.", "This app already has 100 keys, the most it can keep. Remove one to add another." Replacing a value never counts as a new key, and a smaller value fits in a full app.
- **(2.4) Writes are atomic and race-safe:** a `PUT` or `DELETE` writes the value and the totals in one `TransactWriteItems`, only while the key's size in the totals item is still the one that was read (since try 3, see "Writes never read the value item"; `attribute_not_exists` for a new key) and the totals stay within the limits: `bytes <= 1 MB - change` and `keys <= 100 - new key`, so parallel writes can never pass the limits (tested with 130 parallel writes on the fake, and the conditions on the DynamoDB request). A lost race (a value or totals condition, or `TransactionConflict`) reads again and tries again after a random 20 to 80 ms pause (every write of an app updates the same `DATA_USAGE` item, so simultaneous writes, such as the template's shared counter pressed by several visitors, cancel each other with `TransactionConflict` on DynamoDB, and an immediate retry would collide again), 4 tries in all (try 1 had 3 without a pause), then answers 409 "This value was being changed by someone else at the same moment. Please try again."; a real limit found on the new read answers 413. Two writes of the same size to one key both succeed (last write wins): the totals stay right because they depend only on sizes.
- **(2.4) Logs:** one JSON line per request with method, outcome, status and the value's size; never the nickname, key, value, headers or event; failures log only the error name and answer 500 "The data service had a problem. Please try again in a moment."
- **(2.4) Daily write budget (try 2, after the access review; size and count changed in try 3):** replacing a value never meets the 1 MB or 100-key limits, so a loop of 100 KB `PUT`s on one key at the stage's 50 requests/s would cost hundreds of dollars a day in DynamoDB writes (about 2 write units per KB in a transaction). Each app may write 5 MB per UTC day (try 2: 20 MB), counted as DynamoDB bills it (try 3, `writeCost` in `platform/api/app-data/data.ts`): both items the transaction writes, each in whole KB at the larger of its old and new size (DynamoDB bills the larger), so the value item counts `max(bytes, previousBytes)` plus 256 bytes of item overhead, at least 1 KB (a `DELETE` counts the value it removes), and the totals item counts its own size (256 bytes plus each key's length and 8 bytes, ~7.3 KB with 100 keys of 64 characters). A small write counts 2 KB, a 100 KB one 102 KB, and the "PUT 100 KB, then PUT 1" trick counts 102 KB for each write (try 2 counted 1 KB for the second; tested). A `DELETE` of a missing key, reads and refused writes count nothing. 5 MB is about 2,500 small writes a day per app (a press of the template's counter is one), ample for a workshop app, while a loop of 100 KB writes stops after 50; at worst 30 live apps x 5 MB x 2 write units per KB is ~0.3 M write units a day, about $0.25 a day, ~$7 a month (on-demand eu-central-1 price assumed: $0.7625 per million write units). The count lives in the `DATA_USAGE` item (`writeDay` = UTC date YYYY-MM-DD, `writtenBytes`) and changes in the same `TransactWriteItems` as the value, so it is race-safe like the 1 MB limit: when the read showed writes of today, the update adds the cost with the condition `writeDay = :today AND writtenBytes <= budget - cost`; otherwise it sets `writeDay = :today, writtenBytes = cost` with the condition `attribute_not_exists(writeDay) OR writeDay < :today` (try 3; try 2 had `<>`; ISO dates compare as text): a write of today that got in first makes it a lost race, read again, and a stored day later than today (another instance's clock ahead) also fails the condition, so the count only moves forward and is never reset by a clock behind (tested; such a write answers 409 after its 4 tries, for the few moments the clocks differ). Over budget answers 429 "This app has saved a lot of data today. Saving starts again tomorrow." (outcome `write-budget`), storing nothing; reads keep working. The day turns at midnight UTC.
- **(2.4) Route throttles (try 2; writes raised in try 3):** instead of the stage's default 50 requests/s (burst 100), `PUT` and `DELETE /api/apps/{nickname}/data/{key}` get 30 requests/s (burst 60; try 2 had 10/20, reachable by a workshop room of ~30 people pressing shared counters) and `GET` 20/s (burst 40), as stage `RouteSettings` like the sign-in routes; `OPTIONS` also 20/s (burst 40) since the final access review (each preflight invokes the function; browsers keep the answer 600 s, so a real app sends few). The throttles are for the whole API (all apps together), the budget per app. A read is not much cheaper than a write per request: a `GET` of a 100 KB value costs 13 read units (eventually consistent, 4 KB per unit, half price) plus 1 for the live marker, ~$2 per million `GET`s, and ~100 KB of CloudFront egress (~$8.70 per million); a write refused with 429 costs 3 read units (live marker, totals item) and its API Gateway and Lambda requests. With the budget capping write units and a write reading only the small totals item, 30 writes/s is cheap; see "Remaining worst-case cost".
- **(2.4) Writes never read the value item (try 3, after the access re-review):** DynamoDB bills a `GetItem` on the whole item's size even with a `ProjectionExpression`, so try 2's consistent read of the value item (to learn its previous size) cost up to 25 read units on every `PUT` and `DELETE`, refused ones included, for a stranger who stored one 100 KB value. Each key's size now lives in the totals item's map `sizes` (`SET sizes.#k = :size`, `REMOVE sizes.#k`; the data key goes through `ExpressionAttributeNames`, as keys may hold dashes; IAM's `dynamodb:Attributes` holds only top-level names, so it sees `sizes`, per the DynamoDB guide). A write reads only the totals item (consistent, ≤ 2 read units) and checks the limits (413) and the budget (429) from it before anything else is read or written; the first write of an app creates the item with the map (`SET sizes = :sizes`, condition `attribute_not_exists(pk)`), since a nested `SET` on a missing map fails. The value item's own condition is dropped: the transaction's one condition is on the totals item (`sizes.#k = :previous`, or `attribute_exists(sizes) AND attribute_not_exists(sizes.#k)` for a new key, plus the limits and the budget), and since every change to a value changes its `sizes` entry in the same transaction, and transactions are serializable, the entry always holds the stored value's size: a write that read a stale size fails the condition, reads again and tries again, as before. Two writes of the same size to one key both succeed (last write wins), as before. No migration: items written by tries 1 and 2 never reached production (nothing was deployed).
- **(2.4) `GET` reads eventually consistently (try 3):** half the cost of a consistent read. A read right after a write may, rarely (typically for under a second), return the previous value; acceptable for apps. The template's counter reads only when the page loads, so a visitor would need to reload within that moment to see the previous count (a press then adds to it, and one press is lost); writes are unaffected (they read the totals item consistently). The live marker stays a consistent read (1 read unit).
- **(2.4) CloudFront `/api/apps/*` adds CORS to every data API answer (try 3, after the checker):** API Gateway's own answers (a route throttle's 429, and any answer made before the function) carry no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, so a sandboxed app could not read them and `workshop-data.js` showed "The data service could not be reached. Check the connection and try again." The edge stack has a behaviour `/api/apps/*`, before `/api/*`, identical to it (same origin, all methods, HTTPS only, CachingDisabled, AllViewerExceptHostHeader) but with a custom response headers policy `ssd-workshop-app-data-v1` (retained, versioned like the pages' policy; the deploy rights can create response headers policies) that keeps the managed SecurityHeadersPolicy's headers (HSTS one year, nosniff, `SAMEORIGIN`, XSS block, referrer, none overriding) and adds CORS: `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *`, no credentials, methods `GET, PUT, DELETE`, headers `content-type, accept`, max age 600 (the function's preflight values), `originOverride: true` so the function's own header is replaced and an answer never carries it twice (CloudFront adds CORS headers to requests that send `Origin`, which every sandboxed fetch does: `Origin: null`). A throttled visitor then sees the API's own `message`: API Gateway's throttle body is `{"message":"Too Many Requests"}`, so "Too Many Requests"; without a readable body `workshop-data.js` shows "The data service answered with an error (429)." (`templates/basic/workshop-data.js` unchanged; to confirm after the deploy, infra/README.md).
- **(2.4) Remaining worst-case cost (after the final access review):** a deliberate attack that holds all four data API routes at their throttles for 24 hours, against 30 live apps, with 100 KB values stored to read back. Prices assumed (eu-central-1, on demand): $0.1525 per million read units, $0.7625 per million write units, API Gateway HTTP $1.11 per million requests, Lambda arm64 ~$0.35 per million short invocations, CloudWatch Logs ~$0.63 per GB, CloudFront $1.20 per million HTTPS requests, $0.085 per GB out and $0.02 per GB to the origin. Requests a day: `GET` 20/s and `OPTIONS` 20/s, 1.73 M each; `PUT` and `DELETE` 30/s each, 5.18 M together; 8.64 M in all. Specific to this API, ~$26 a day: DynamoDB ~$11 (writes capped by the budget $0.23; reads by writes up to 9 read units each when an attacker forces lost races, $7.10; reads by `GET`s, 14 read units each, $3.70), API Gateway $9.60, Lambda $3, logs (~0.5 KB a request) $2.70. Shared with any public page or route (CloudFront request fees and egress, which also apply to the site's own files, with no throttle at all on cached pages): requests $10.40, `GET` egress ~177 GB $15, `PUT` bodies to the origin ~265 GB $5.30, ~$31 a day. In all ~$57 a day, ~$1,700 a month if sustained for a whole month (~$800 of it this API's own). Not counted, to measure after the deploy (`ReturnConsumedCapacity` on a cancelled transaction): whether DynamoDB bills write units for transactions cancelled by a lost race; if so, a forced-race attack adds up to a few dollars per app per day. Without an attacker, a workshop day costs cents. Above the $50 a month threshold only under a deliberate, sustained attack: Franck's call (Stop-and-ask 3), e.g. accept it with an AWS Budgets alert, lower the throttles, or put AWS WAF rate limits per address in front (a new paid service, ~$6 a month plus $0.60 per million requests). **Franck accepted it on 2026-11-24, with an AWS Budgets alert he sets.** Proposed follow-up before the workshop (needs his yes, new paid service): an AWS WAF rate rule per address on `/api/apps/*` (a single attacker can otherwise use up the shared throttles and stop every app saving) and Cost Anomaly Detection; for phase 5, apps' data read-only a few days after the workshop.
- **(2.4) The in-memory fake does not reproduce `TransactionConflict`** (two transactions on one item at the same moment cancel each other even when both conditions hold): it only fails conditions, which the code handles the same way. The pause and the 4 tries are sized for the real table and checked only after the deploy.
- **(2.5) Apps stack:** `ssd-workshop-apps` (eu-central-1, `infra/lib/apps-stack.ts`, assets-bucket synthesizer for the bundled deployer) holds the apps bucket, the deploy queue and its dead-letter queue, and the deployer Lambda. It depends on the assets stack, and the edge stack depends on it (it serves the bucket). It finds the table by name (`TABLE_NAME`) and the distribution by `DISTRIBUTION_ID`: no cross-stack reference. `cdk deploy --all` in the deploy workflow deploys it; its name matches the access stack's `stack/ssd-workshop-*` rule, and every right its deploy needs (S3, SQS, Lambda, CloudFront functions and policies) is already in the deploy policies: no access-stack change.
- **(2.5) Apps bucket:** `ssd-workshop-apps-<account>` (S3 names are global, like the site bucket's), private (all public access blocked), S3-managed encryption, TLS only, ACLs off, retained. Keys are `apps/<lower-cased nickname>/<path>`, so the CloudFront path maps straight to the key (no origin path). Its policy lets only the workshop's distribution `s3:GetObject` (`AWS:SourceArn` equal to `DISTRIBUTION_ARN`, not `distribution/*`; try 2) and **denies** every object-changing action (`PutObject`, `DeleteObject`, `DeleteObjectVersion`, the object tagging, ACL, retention and legal hold writes, `RestoreObject`, `ReplicateObject`, `ReplicateDelete`; `APPS_OBJECT_WRITE_ACTIONS`) to every principal whose `aws:PrincipalArn` is not the deployer's role, the deploy role and the Claude user included. Bucket-level settings are not denied, so deploys still manage the bucket (and could change the policy: that is the deploy rights' trust, as for every resource). The deny stops direct writes only: the deploy role and the Claude user also hold `sqs:*`, `lambda:*` and `dynamodb:*` on `ssd-workshop-*`, so either could write a `queued` build and send a deploy message (or invoke the deployer with a made-up event) and have any commit of an app's repository published. Operators are trusted, as for every resource; narrowing that (a deny on `sqs:SendMessage` to the deploy queue and `lambda:InvokeFunction` on the deployer for both principals) is an access-stack change, proposed to Franck as a follow-up with the boundary's S3 narrowing.
- **(2.5) Bucket-level actions, an accepted-by-design gap (try 2, access review M2):** the deny covers object actions only; a deny on the bucket's policy or lifecycle actions could lock CloudFormation out of the bucket. Workload roles' boundary allows `s3:*` on `ssd-workshop-*`, so the deny holds only as long as no workload role is granted `s3:PutBucketPolicy`, `s3:DeleteBucketPolicy`, `s3:PutLifecycleConfiguration`, `s3:PutBucketWebsite`, `s3:PutBucketCORS` or `s3:DeleteBucket` on the apps bucket: a synth test fails when any IAM policy in any stack (the deployer's included) grants one of them, or a wildcard covering it, on a resource that can reach the apps bucket. Proposed follow-up for Franck (access stack, not this ticket): narrow the boundary's S3 statement so workload roles get no bucket-level writes at all.
- **(2.5) Both forms of the deployer's role ARN in the deny:** `arn:aws:iam::<account>:role/ssd-workshop/ssd-workshop-apps-deployer` and the same without the path (decision "(2.2) Boundary RunBuilds pinned": unverified whether `aws:PrincipalArn` carries the path). Role names are unique in an account whatever the path, so both name only the deployer's role. Check after the deploy: the first deploy publishes (the role passes) and `aws s3 cp` with the Claude user is refused (infra/README.md).
- **(2.5) Deploy queue:** FIFO `ssd-workshop-deploys.fifo`, content-based deduplication off (the sender, ticket 2.8, gives the build id as deduplication id and the lower-cased nickname as message group, so an app's deploys run one at a time and in order), SQS-managed encryption, visibility 150 s (the deployer's 120 s timeout plus 30 s), 1 day retention, 3 deliveries, then the FIFO dead-letter queue `ssd-workshop-deploys-dlq.fifo` (14 days). Event source: batch size 1, at most 5 deployers at once.
- **(2.5) Deploy message:** JSON with exactly `buildId` (1 to 64 letters, digits, dashes or underscores, `BUILD_ID_PATTERN`), `nickname` (the 1.5 rules, as typed) and `commit` (40 lower-case hex digits); any other shape, an extra field included, is logged as `invalid` (no values) and dropped (not retried). **Commit binding (MANDATORY for 2.8/2.9; try 2, made mandatory in try 3, access review 3):** the deployer publishes whatever commit the message names, so the commit must come only from the platform's own push result (the commit the builder pushed to the app's repository), never from participant input. Tickets 2.8/2.9 MUST (1) have the builder store the commit it pushed on the build (`pushedCommit`), and (2) add `AND #pushedCommit = :commit` to the deployer's start condition (and `pushedCommit` to `DEPLOYER_BUILD_UPDATE_ATTRIBUTES`, since IAM counts condition names), in that ticket. Why it matters: a commit SHA alone does not prove the commit came from the participant's repository, since GitHub serves any commit of the fork network through the parent repository's address (`codeload.github.com/<parent>/tar.gz/<sha>` answers for a fork's commit). Also for 2.9: a build left in `deploying` after its message reached the dead-letter queue (the last delivery could not even mark it failed) is moved to `failed` by the launcher's stuck-build check.
- **(2.5) Deployer:** `ssd-workshop-apps-deployer`, Node.js 22 arm64, 512 MB, 120 s, own role and log group (two weeks), code in `platform/api/deploy/` and `platform/api/handlers/deployer.ts`. Per message: mark the build `deploying`, conditional on `attribute_exists(pk) AND #nickname = :nickname AND #status IN (:queued, :building, :deploying)` (try 2, access review M1: the build exists, is the message's app's, and still waits for its deploy; `deploying` lets a redelivery after a crash go on); when the condition fails (missing build, another app's, already `live` or `failed`) the message is logged `no-build` and dropped, before any download, and nothing is written; a failure is recorded on a build only after this step passed; download the tarball anonymously from `https://codeload.github.com/ssd-workshop-apps/ssd-<lower-cased nickname>/tar.gz/<commit>` (public repositories; ticket 2.6 may add a token); unpack; run `checkApp`; on a pass put every file, remove the keys the new version no longer has, invalidate `/apps/<nickname>/*`, write the live marker, mark the build `live`.
- **(2.5) Download and unpack limits:** at most 10 MB downloaded (`MAX_DOWNLOAD_BYTES`, the announced length checked first, the stream dropped past it), within 30 s; at most 8 MB unpacked (`MAX_UNPACKED_BYTES`: the 5 MB an app may hold plus room for tar headers and padding; `gunzipSync` with `maxOutputLength` stops there, so a "zip bomb" never fills memory). Past either limit the build fails at once with a plain reason ("The repository is over 8 MB unpacked; an app may hold at most 5 MB, so nothing was published.").
- **(2.5) Tar reader without a dependency** (`platform/api/deploy/tar.ts`, ~150 lines, tested): ustar with the prefix field, GNU long names (`L`, `K` skipped), pax `x` records (`path`, `size`), pax global headers (`g`, GitHub's `pax_global_header`) skipped, checksums checked, base-256 sizes refused. The single top folder is stripped (anything outside one top folder fails: "does not hold one folder at its top"). Hard links and symlinks become `symlink`, folders `dir`, plain and contiguous files `file`, everything else `other` (decision "(2.3) For 2.5"). A path that is absolute or holds `..` fails the whole archive before anything else ("…leaves its folder…"). When a path appears twice, the last entry wins, as tar does.
- **(2.5) Check failures change nothing live:** nothing is put or removed, nothing invalidated, the live marker untouched; the build gets `status: "failed"`, `failureReasons` (each problem as `<file>, line <n>: <reason>`, `<file>: <reason>` or the reason alone, in the check's order) and `finishedAt`. Each reason at most 500 characters and all together at most 16,000 (`MAX_REASON_CHARS`, `MAX_REASONS_CHARS`), with a closing "…and N more problems." line when cut: the build item stays far under DynamoDB's 400 KB. The reasons quote file names and lines from anyone's repository: before storing, every control, format (bidirectional overrides, zero-width), private-use, unassigned or lone surrogate character and every line or paragraph separator becomes `?` (`storedReason`, try 2, access review M3). Pages (2.7, 2.10) must render `failureReasons` with `textContent` only, never as HTML.
- **(2.5) Passing problems:** an error from GitHub (any answer but 200, no answer, timeout) or from AWS is logged by name only and rethrown as a bare `deploy failed`, so SQS delivers the message again; on the last delivery (`ApproximateReceiveCount` = 3, `DEPLOY_MAX_RECEIVES`) the build is marked failed instead, with GitHub's reason or "The workshop could not publish the app because of a problem on its side. Please try again.". Only when even that write fails (logged `dead-letter`), or the error came before the build passed its start condition (logged `dead-letter`, the build left alone), does the message reach the dead-letter queue.
- **(2.5) The Lambda's deadline (try 2, checker 1):** the handler passes now + the context's `getRemainingTimeInMillis()` to `processDeploy`. No phase (the download, each batch of 8 puts, the listing, the removal, the invalidation, the live marker, the live mark) starts with less than `DEPLOY_RESERVE_MS` (15 s) left, and each phase is cut off when only that much is left (the download's own time limit is the time left plus 1 s, at most 30 s); the cut-off is a passing problem (`DeployTimeUp`): retried before the last delivery, and on the last one the build is marked failed with "The deploy took too long." within the 15 s left (the table client's own timeouts: 2 tries of 1.5 s). Each phase gets its own `AbortController`, aborted when its time is up, and passes the signal to every call it makes (`client.send(command, { abortSignal })` for S3, CloudFront and DynamoDB, the download's `fetch`), so a cut-off call stops instead of going on in the background (try 3, access review M1). Belt and braces, the build's last writes (`live`, `failed`) are conditional on `#status = :deploying` (`FINISH_CONDITION`), so a late write can never change a finished build; a `live` mark whose condition fails (another writer finished the build meanwhile) is logged `no-build` and leaves the build as it is. After a time-up part-way through the upload, the live app may mix files of two versions (some new, some old) until the next successful deploy; the build is marked failed, and the recovery is a new build or `npm run builds -- retry <nickname>` (2.9). Without a context (the bundle test), no deadline. A 404 from GitHub is retried too (it can be a passing state right after a push), then reported as "GitHub answered 404 …".
- **(2.5) Publishing order and caching:** files are put 8 at a time, every non-page file first and the `.html` pages last, so a new page never names a file that is not there yet; stale keys are removed after the puts, then `/apps/<nickname>/*` is invalidated (caller reference `<build id>-<ms>`, one wildcard path: within CloudFront's free 1,000 paths a month for a workshop). For a few seconds a visitor may see old and new files mixed. A failed or timed-out upload part-way (passing problem, or the deadline) leaves the live app with files of two versions until the next successful deploy, while the live marker and the build show no new commit; the recovery is a new build or `retry` (2.9). **Order of the live writes (try 3, checker 4):** the live marker first, then the build `live`. If the build's write fails after the marker's, the build stays `deploying`: SQS delivers again, the deploy runs again (same files, the marker keeps its first `liveAt`) and marks it live, or the last delivery marks it failed, while the marker (which opens the app's data API) truly says the app's files are served. The other order could leave a `live` build with no marker, which no redelivery repairs (the start condition refuses a live build) and which keeps the app's data API answering 404 until another build. Tested. `Cache-Control: public, max-age=60` on every file, like the pages.
- **(2.5) Content types** from the extension in any letter case: `.html` `text/html; charset=utf-8`, `.css` `text/css; charset=utf-8`, `.js` `text/javascript; charset=utf-8`, `.json` `application/json; charset=utf-8`, `.md` `text/markdown; charset=utf-8`, `.txt` `text/plain; charset=utf-8`, `.svg` `image/svg+xml`, `.png` `image/png`, `.jpg`/`.jpeg` `image/jpeg`, `.gif` `image/gif`, `.webp` `image/webp`, `.ico` `image/x-icon`, `.woff` `font/woff`, `.woff2` `font/woff2` (a test checks every allowed extension has one).
- **(2.5) Build record:** `BUILD#<build id>` / `BUILD` (`buildKey`, `BuildItem` in `platform/api/types.ts`: `type: "build"`, `buildId`, `nickname`, `status` `queued | building | deploying | live | failed`, `updatedAt`, and the deployer's `deployedCommit`, `failureReasons`, `finishedAt`). Ticket 2.7 creates builds and stores `nickname` exactly as the message will carry it (as the participant typed it; the deployer's start condition compares it exactly, since DynamoDB cannot compare without case); the deployer only updates existing ones (`UpdateItem`, condition `attribute_exists(pk)`, the start condition above, and since try 3 `#status = :deploying` for `live` and `failed`): `live` sets `deployedCommit` and removes `failureReasons`, `failed` sets `failureReasons` and removes `deployedCommit`, both set `finishedAt`. The `APP#` partition keeps only the live marker.
- **(2.5) Live marker write:** `UpdateItem` of `APP#<lower-cased nickname>` / `LIVE`, `SET type = "app-live", nickname = <as typed in the message>, liveAt = if_not_exists(liveAt, now)`: created on the first successful deploy, `liveAt` kept on later ones. Written after the invalidation and before the build is marked live.
- **(2.5) Deployer rights** (tested on the synth output): its logs; `s3:PutObject` and `s3:DeleteObject` on `apps/*` of the apps bucket; `s3:ListBucket` on the bucket with `s3:prefix` `apps/*`; `cloudfront:CreateInvalidation` on the live distribution only (`DISTRIBUTION_ARN`); `dynamodb:UpdateItem` in `APP#*` naming only `pk sk type nickname liveAt` (`DEPLOYER_LIVE_UPDATE_ATTRIBUTES`) and in `BUILD#*` naming only `pk sk status nickname updatedAt finishedAt deployedCommit failureReasons` (`DEPLOYER_BUILD_UPDATE_ATTRIBUTES`; `nickname` since try 2, because IAM's `dynamodb:Attributes` counts the names a condition uses: the deployer only compares it. IAM cannot tell a compared name from a set one, so the role could also set `nickname` on a `BUILD#` item; accepted like the stray `APP#` items below, and a test checks that no deployer `UpdateExpression` sets or removes `#nickname`, or removes `#status` or sets it to anything but `deploying`, `live` or `failed` (try 3)); both table statements also require `dynamodb:ReturnValues` in `NONE UPDATED_OLD UPDATED_NEW` (`StringEqualsIfExists`, try 2); consuming its queue. No GetItem, PutItem or DeleteItem, nothing in `APPDATA#*`. IAM cannot condition on the sort key, so the role could also create or update other `APP#<x>` items (and other `BUILD#<x>` sort keys) with the allowed attributes; accepted by design (only the deployer's own code holds the role, and readers key on `LIVE` / `BUILD`). A synth test checks that no other IAM policy in any stack has a write right that reaches the apps bucket (by name, logical id or wildcard), and that the site's `BucketDeployment` targets the site bucket only. Another (try 2) checks that no IAM policy in any stack may `sqs:SendMessage` (or `sqs:Send*`, `sqs:*`, `*`) to the deploy queue (by name, ARN, logical id or wildcard); ticket 2.9 adds the builder as the one sender and updates that test.
- **(2.5) `DISTRIBUTION_ID = "E2TOZV0ERAXKZ9"`** in `infra/lib/config.ts` and `platform/api/settings.ts` (kept equal by a test), the live distribution, a constant like `API_ID`: recreating the edge stack means updating both.
- **(2.5) Two new AWS SDK clients** in `platform`: `@aws-sdk/client-s3` and `@aws-sdk/client-cloudfront` (`^3.1176.0`, same release line as the others): the deployer puts, lists and deletes objects and creates invalidations; bundled like the rest (`externalModules: []`).
- **(2.5) CloudFront `/apps/*`:** a third origin, the apps bucket's regional endpoint through a new origin access control `ssd-workshop-apps` (retained); behaviour `/apps/*` after `/api/apps/*` and `/api/*` (it cannot match an `/api/` path), GET and HEAD, `REDIRECT_TO_HTTPS`, managed `CachingOptimized`, the new response headers policy `ssd-workshop-apps-v1` (retained, versioned like the pages' policy: `APPS_CONTENT_SECURITY_POLICY` overriding, nosniff, HSTS one year, referrer `strict-origin-when-cross-origin`; no `X-Frame-Options`: the sandbox already gives the app an opaque origin, and the brief asks for none; can be added with a version bump) and a viewer-request CloudFront Function.
- **(2.5) CloudFront Function `ssd-workshop-apps-index`** (runtime `cloudfront-js-2.0`, code in `infra/lib/apps-index.js`, tested by evaluating the file): `/apps/<nickname>` without a slash answers 301 to `/apps/<nickname>/` (relative links in the page then resolve inside the app); a nickname with upper-case letters answers 301 to its lower-case address (the rest of the path keeps its case); the query string is kept on a redirect; a path ending in `/` asks for `index.html`. 301 (permanent) because both mappings never change. CloudFront Functions cost $0.10 per million calls, a feature of CloudFront (already approved), not a new service. CloudFormation tags the function (`AWS::CloudFront::Function` takes `Tags`); the deploy rights cover it through `CloudFrontWorkshopFunctions` and `TagOnCreateThroughCloudFormation`, unverified until the first deploy.
- **(2.5) 404 page:** S3 behind an origin access control answers a missing key with 403 (CloudFront may not list the bucket). The distribution turns every 403 into `/404.html` (from the site bucket: `platform/web/404.html`, published by the site's build through `STATIC_FILES`) with status 404, cached 60 s. It holds no script and no stylesheet ("Page not found … This page does not exist. Check the address, or ask your facilitator for the right one." and a link to the workshop), so it shows the same under the apps' CSP and the pages'. A missing page of the site itself now gets this page too (it was S3's XML 403 before). The API's functions never answer 403 (no `403` or `Forbidden` in any non-test source of `platform/api`, checked by `platform/api/statuses.test.ts` since try 2), and the pages only test for 404, so nothing else changes; a 403 that API Gateway made itself (none is expected without authorizers) would now also show this page, with status 404.
- **(2.5) Logs:** one JSON line per message: `event: "deployer"`, `outcome` (`live`, `failed`, `invalid`, `no-build`, `retry`, `gave-up`, `dead-letter`), the file count, bytes, removed keys, the number of problems and the duration, plus an error name for `retry`/`gave-up`/`dead-letter` (`no-build` also when a failure or live write found the build already finished, so nothing was written; a failed step also aborts the calls it started alongside); never a nickname, commit, build id, file name, reason or error message (the reasons are stored on the build, where 2.7's page reads them).
- **(2.6) Secret home and shape:** `ssd-workshop-github` (`GITHUB_SECRET_NAME` in `infra/lib/config.ts` and `platform/api/settings.ts`, kept equal by a test) lives in the builds stack (`ssd-workshop-builds`, which build launches need; no new stack). CDK gives no value, so CloudFormation stores a random placeholder until Franck loads `{"appId": "<id>", "privateKey": "<PEM text>"}` (command in `infra/README.md`). Retained, tagged, default `aws/secretsmanager` key, no resource policy (denied), no rotation (GitHub keys are rotated by hand), and no role may read it yet (synth test; 2.9 adds the build launcher as the one reader and updates that test). Cost: one secret, $0.40 a month plus $0.05 per 10,000 reads, a service the approved brief names. The secret's properties are pinned whole by a test: unverified whether a CloudFormation update of them (a new description, say) could regenerate the value, so they must change only deliberately, and Franck reloads the value if they do.
- **(2.6) Installation looked up, not stored:** the platform signs the app's JWT (RS256 with `node:crypto`, issued 60 s back, valid 9 minutes, `iss` the app id) and asks `GET /orgs/ssd-workshop-apps/installation`, so Franck loads only the app id and key. An installation on any other account is refused. GitHub is called with plain `fetch` (`https://api.github.com`, `X-GitHub-Api-Version: 2022-11-28`, `User-Agent: ssd-workshop-platform`, redirects refused, 10 s per call, an optional abort signal); no GitHub library.
- **(2.6) A secret that is not the expected JSON** (CDK's placeholder, a key alone, an id that is not digits) fails with "The GitHub secret is not loaded yet: …", and a key `node:crypto` cannot read (or not RSA) with "The GitHub secret's private key cannot be read: …"; neither quotes the value (`JSON.parse`'s own message would, so it is dropped). The app id may be given as text or a number.
- **(2.6) Two tokens for the platform's own work:** creating or finding the repository uses an installation token asking only `administration: write` (it reaches the organization's repositories, as creating one needs); writing the first commit uses a token for that one repository with `contents: write`, the same kind as the builder's. Both are minted per call and never stored. The organization-wide token is used only when GitHub's answer grants exactly `administration: write` (plus `metadata: read`), else "GitHub's token grants more than administration write …" (try 2). **For 2.9:** that token reaches every repository of the organization, so it stays inside the launcher process that minted it: never stored, queued, logged or passed to the builder task (which gets only `builderToken`'s one-repository token).
- **(2.6) Repository creation:** `POST /orgs/ssd-workshop-apps/repos` with `name: ssd-<lower-cased nickname>` (`appRepositoryName`, after checking the nickname against the 1.5 rules, `NICKNAME_PATTERN`: 3 to 20 letters, digits or dashes always make a valid GitHub name), `visibility: public`, `private: false`, no issues, projects or wiki, no `auto_init`, description "An app made at the SSD Workshop" (no nickname or participant text). A 422 (a taken name) is followed by `GET /repos/...`: reused when it is in the organization and public; a private one fails with "The app's repository is not public; make it public in GitHub or delete it, then try again." (the deployer downloads anonymously); a 422 for a repository that does not exist fails plainly.
- **(2.6) Default branch `main`:** the repository's `default_branch` must be `main` (GitHub takes it from the organization's setting, default `main`); otherwise the call fails with "…set the organization's default branch to main." rather than renaming branches. Unverified live: that an empty repository reports the organization's default as `default_branch`.
- **(2.6) First commit:** GitHub's Git Data API answers 409 "Git Repository is empty" for an empty repository, so one Contents API commit (`README.md`, "This repository is being prepared by the SSD Workshop.", message "Open the repository for the workshop") opens it, then a parentless commit holding exactly the template and `SPEC.md` (blobs in base64, one tree without `base_tree`, files mode `100644`) replaces it on `main` (forced). The history therefore starts with the template and the spec; the opening commit stays readable by its id on GitHub but holds only that fixed line. A repository whose `main` is still that opening commit alone (an earlier try stopped half-way) is treated as new; see "(2.6) Leftover opening commit".
- **(2.6) Rebuild:** when the repository exists with history, a new commit on top of `main` (fast-forward only, not forced) holds the template and the new `SPEC.md`: a rebuild after `conversation reset` starts from the template, the earlier app stays in the history. Messages are fixed: "Start from the workshop template and the approved spec" (first) and "Start again from the workshop template and the newly approved spec" (rebuild).
- **(2.6) Commit identity:** author and committer of every commit the platform makes (and, in 2.8, the builder's) are `SSD Workshop builder <noreply@workshop.rankly.games>` (`BUILDER_COMMIT_NAME`, `BUILDER_COMMIT_EMAIL`): an address on the workshop's own domain with no mailbox, which no GitHub account can verify, rather than the app's `[bot]` noreply address (unknown until the app's user id is read). Messages are fixed text (except change commits, see "(3.7) The change commit"); the spec reaches only `SPEC.md`, with its email addresses removed (decision "(2.6) Email addresses removed from SPEC.md").
- **(2.6) Builder's token:** `builderToken(credentials, nickname)` asks `POST /app/installations/<id>/access_tokens` for `repositories: ["ssd-<nickname>"]` and `permissions: {contents: "write"}` and refuses (never returns) an answer whose expiry is past or more than one hour plus one minute of clock drift away (`MAX_TOKEN_LIFETIME_MS`, `TOKEN_CLOCK_SKEW_MS`), whose selection is not `selected`, that lists another or more repositories, or that grants anything but `contents: write` and `metadata: read` (GitHub adds the latter to every token). It returns the token, `expires_at` and `owner/name`. No `workflows` right: the builder cannot change GitHub Actions files (the repositories run none).
- **(2.6) Logs:** one JSON line per GitHub call, `event: "github"`, the step (`installation`, `installation-token`, `create-repository`, `get-repository`, `get-branch`, `get-commit`, `get-tree`, `bootstrap`, `blob`, `tree`, `commit`, `update-branch`, `app`), GitHub's status (or `unreachable` with the error's name) and the duration; never a token, the JWT, the key, the nickname, the repository name, a path or GitHub's answer. Errors are `GitHubProblem`s with a plain reason naming the step and status only.
- **(2.6) Template files are an input:** `prepareRepository` takes the template's files (`{ path, bytes }`); `loadTemplateFiles()` reads `templates/basic` (plain files only) for scripts and tests. **For 2.9:** the launcher Lambda has no repository folder, so its bundle must carry `templates/basic` (for example a module generated at synth time, or esbuild's `commandHooks` copying the folder next to `index.js`) and pass the same list; a synth test should check the bundled files equal the folder's.
- **(2.6) `smoke github`:** reads the secret with the caller's AWS credentials (Franck's profile, `eu-central-1`), signs the JWT and prints the app's name, slug and owner, the installation's account, its type, its id, the repository selection and the rights, in that order; it mints no installation token and prints no token, JWT or key. Unit-tested against the fake GitHub; unverified live until Franck runs it. Node's `fetch` ignores `HTTPS_PROXY`, so it runs from Franck's machine, not a session.
- **(2.6) New dependency `@aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager`** (`^3.1176.0`, the same release line as the other AWS clients) in `platform`, for `smoke github` now and the launcher (2.9).
- **(2.6) The GitHub App's key locked away (Franck, 2026-11-24, PR #31):** the deploy role and the Claude user hold `secretsmanager:*` on `ssd-workshop-*` (`NamedWorkshopResources`), so a loaded key would let Claude sessions and deploys change any repository of `ssd-workshop-apps` (and, through the deployer, any live app). Two denies, applied by Franck: (1) `NoGitHubKey` (in `ssd-workshop-deploy-read`, because `ssd-workshop-deploy` is too close to IAM's 6,144-character limit for it; both principals carry that policy) denies the deploy principals every Secrets Manager action on `secret:ssd-workshop-github-*` except `CreateSecret`, `DescribeSecret`, `TagResource`, `UntagResource`, `GetResourcePolicy`, `ListSecretVersionIds` and `ValidateResourcePolicy` (a `NotAction` list, so actions AWS adds later are denied too; checked against the service reference's 27 actions, read 2026-11-24): CloudFormation still creates the secret; any later change of the secret resource (its settings or its tags, since CloudFormation's update handler reads the value) and drift detection on it fail for the deploy principals and go through Franck. (2) `GitHubKeyForTheLauncherOnly` in the `ssd-workshop-boundary` permissions boundary denies `GetSecretValue` on it to every workload role but `ssd-workshop-builds-launcher` (both ARN forms, decision "(2.2)"), so no other role (created by a session or a deploy) can read it. The builder task role (2.8) never gets it. **Sessions kept off the launcher (Franck, 2026-11-24: "Block sessions, trust deploys"):** the boundary exempts the launcher role, so a principal that can create, pass, change or run that role (or the launcher's function or code) could reach the key through it. `NoLauncherForSessions` (in `ssd-workshop-deploy-read`, conditioned on `aws:PrincipalArn` = the Claude user) denies the Claude user everything but reads on the role `ssd-workshop-builds-launcher` (both ARN forms), the function `ssd-workshop-builds-launcher` (with its versions and aliases; invoking included) and the CDK assets bucket and its objects (a planted asset with the next deploy's hash would become the launcher's code, since cdk-assets skips an asset that exists; templates are there too). `NoDeployAssetWrites` in the boundary denies every workload role everything but reads on that bucket and its objects, so a role a session creates can neither plant an asset nor open the bucket with a bucket policy. **Accepted by Franck:** the deploy role can still do all of this; it runs only from `main`, so only merged code reaches the key, guarded by his review. **For 2.9 (must):** the launcher is a zip Lambda named `ssd-workshop-builds-launcher` with role `ssd-workshop-builds-launcher` on `/ssd-workshop/` (add a test that the deny names its config constants); no container image and no `ssd-workshop-*` layer (the Claude user can write ECR repositories and layers), or the deny must cover them; never the key or a token in its environment (`lambda:Get*` stays readable); every launcher input (its queue, event source mappings a session may create) is untrusted, so the nickname comes only from the stored build record. Keep `sts:GetFederationToken` ungranted (a federated session would not match the deny's `aws:PrincipalArn`). **Order:** merge, deploy, Franck applies the access stack, then loads the key (`infra/README.md`, "GitHub App secret"). Tested in `access-stack.test.ts` ("the GitHub App's key"). No session read the secret.
- **(2.6) Email addresses removed from SPEC.md (Franck, 2026-11-24, PR #31):** the approved spec goes into a public repository, so before it is committed its control characters other than tab, line feed and carriage return are removed (since 3.7 try 3, so none can split an address), it is put in Unicode's composed form (NFC), the HTML entities GitHub renders as `@` or `.` are read as those characters, and every email-shaped text (a local part, `@`, `＠` or `﹫`, a domain with a dot and a final label of two letters or more or a punycode `xn--` label; letters and marks of any script, full-width forms, invisible format characters anywhere inside) becomes `[email removed]` (`removeEmailAddresses` in `platform/api/github/repository.ts`, used by `withSpec`). Not caught: addresses written with spaces or words ("ada at example dot org"). Also caught, by design: file names shaped like an address (`logo@2x.png`). Linear (a lookbehind lets a match start only where none could start a character earlier; tested on 100,000-character lines). The conversation itself (DynamoDB) keeps the participant's text as written. **Spec delta, CHANGED (Franck, 2026-11-24, his answer on the thread):** ticket 2.6 criterion 1's "the approved spec" in the first commit is the approved spec in composed form with its email addresses removed.
- **(2.6) Git ids checked before use (try 2):** every id GitHub returns that the platform puts into a path or a commit (the branch's commit, the commit's tree, blob, tree and commit ids) must match `^[0-9a-f]{40}$` (`GIT_ID_PATTERN`); otherwise the step fails with "GitHub's answer to the step "<step>" holds no valid id." and the id is never used.
- **(2.6) Leftover opening commit (try 2):** `main` is treated as the platform's own leftover opening commit (and replaced by a forced parentless first commit) only when it has no parent, the opening message, and a tree (not truncated) of exactly `README.md`, mode `100644`, whose blob id is Git's id of `BOOTSTRAP_TEXT` (checked by hash, one `GET /git/trees/<id>`, no blob download). A parentless commit with the opening message but other files or other text is kept: the template commit goes on top of it as a rebuild (fast-forward, not forced), so nothing anyone put there is lost.
- **(2.6) Retry (try 2):** right after a repository is created or opened GitHub briefly answers 404 or 409 on some calls, so every step after `get-branch` (`bootstrap`, `get-commit`, `get-tree`, `blob`, `tree`, `commit`, `update-branch`) is tried at most 3 times, waiting 1 s then 2 s (`RETRY_WAITS_MS`, injected `sleep`), and not again once the caller's signal aborts; any other status (401, 403, 422, 5xx) and an unreachable GitHub are not retried. The first `get-branch` is not retried: its 404 or 409 means an empty repository (its re-read after a 422 to the opening commit is, see "(2.6) Opening refused"). Each try is one log line. These calls are idempotent (Git objects are content-addressed; the branch update names its commit).
- **(2.6) Opening commit checked (try 3):** `get-branch`'s 404 may be GitHub's brief one on a repository that has history, and GitHub's Contents API then puts the opening commit on top of `main`. So the answer to the opening `PUT` is read: `commit.sha` must be a valid id and `commit.parents` a list (else "GitHub's answer to the step "bootstrap" holds no list of parents."). Only an opening commit with no parent leads to the forced parentless first commit; one with a parent is the head of a rebuild (the template commit goes on top of it, fast-forward, not forced), so the earlier app stays on `main` with the opening commit (README.md added) between them.
